T\in\mathrm{SST}_{2n}(\lambda)
, where

(\mathbf{a},T^{\prime}):=d(T).

6.2.
Quantum symmetric pair of type
A\mathrm{II}

For each
i\in[1,n]
, set

B_{2i}:=F_{2i}-q[E_{2i-1},[E_{2i+1},E_{2i}]_{q^{-1}}]_{q^{-1}}K_{2i}^{-1}\in\mathbf{U}.

Here,
[,]_{q^{-1}}
denotes the
q
-commutator given by

[x,y]_{q^{-1}}:=xy-q^{-1}yx.

Let
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
denote the subalgebra of
\mathbf{U}
generated by

\{E_{2i-1},F_{2i-1},K_{2i-1}^{\pm 1}\mid i\in[1,n]\}\sqcup\{B_{2i}\mid i\in[1,n-1]\}.

The pair
(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{U}^{\imath})
forms a quantum symmetric pair of type
A\mathrm{II}_{2n-1}
.

A
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module
M
is said to be a
weight module
if it admits a decomposition

M=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}M_{\mathbf{z}}

as a vector space such that

M_{\mathbf{z}}=\{m\in M\mid K_{2i-1}m=q^{z_{2i-1}-z_{2i}}m\ \text{ for all }i\in[1,n]\}.

The character of a finite-dimensional weight
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module
M
is the Laurent polynomial
\operatorname{ch}_{\imath}M\in\mathbb{Z}[y_{1}^{\pm 1},\dots,y_{n}^{\pm 1}]
given by

\operatorname{ch}_{\imath}M=\sum_{\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}(\dim M_{\mathbf{z}})\mathbf{y}^{\mathbf{z}}.

Proposition 6.2.1
.

Let
M
be a finite-dimensional weight
\mathbf{U}
-module.
Then, we have

\operatorname{ch}_{\imath}M=\operatorname{res}^{\mathfrak{k}}(\operatorname{ch}M).

For each
\nu\in\mathrm{Par}_{\leq n}
, there exists, up to isomorphism, a unique finite-dimensional irreducible
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module
V^{\imath}(\nu)
such that

\operatorname{ch}_{\imath}V^{\imath}(\nu)=s^{Sp}_{\nu}(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})

(
cf.

[
Wat21
, Proposition 3.3.9 and Corollary 4.3.2]
).

The anti-automorphism
\rho
on
\mathbf{U}
restricts to an anti-automorphism on
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
.
The notions of contragredient inner product, almost orthogonal basis, and binary relation
\equiv
are defined for
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-modules as in the same way as
\mathbf{U}
-modules.

Let
M
be a
\mathbf{U}
-module equipped with a contragredient inner product.
When we regard the
\mathbf{U}
-module
M
as a
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module by restriction, the inner product is still contragredient.
In particular, the irreducible
\mathbf{U}
-module
V(\lambda)
, regarded as a
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module, admits a contragredient inner product.
Hence, it is completely reducible.

Proposition 6.2.2
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathrm{Par}_{\leq 2n}
.
Then, the multiplicity of
V^{\imath}(\nu)
in
V(\lambda)
coincides with
m_{\lambda,\nu}
.

Proof.

Let us write

V(\lambda)\simeq\bigoplus_{\nu\in\mathrm{Par}_{\leq n}}V^{\imath}(\nu)^{m^{\prime}_{\lambda,\nu}}

for some
m^{\prime}_{\lambda,\nu}\geq 0
.
Then, we have

\sum_{\nu\in\mathrm{Par}_{\leq n}}m^{\prime}_{\lambda,\nu}s^{Sp}_{\nu}(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})=\operatorname{ch}_{\imath}V(\lambda)=\operatorname{res}^{\mathfrak{k}}(s_{\lambda}(x_{1},\dots,x_{2n}))=\operatorname{ch}_{\mathfrak{k}}V^{\mathfrak{g}}(\lambda),

where the second equality follows from Proposition
6.2.1
.
Now, the assertion follows from Proposition
5.3.2
and the linearly independence of the symplectic Schur functions.
∎

Lemma 6.2.3
.

There exists a
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module isomorphism

K:V(\varpi_{1})\rightarrow V(\varpi_{2n-1})

such that

K(b_{(a)})=b_{s(a)^{\vee}}\ \text{ for all }a\in[1,2n].

Proof.

Clearly, the linear map
K
defined as above is an isomorphism.
Using Example
6.1.1
, one can straightforwardly verify that

K(xb_{(a)})=xb_{s(a)^{\vee}}\ \text{ for all }x\in\mathbf{U}^{\imath}\text{ and }a\in[1,2n].

Hence, the map
K
is a
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module isomorphism.
∎

Lemma 6.2.4
.

There exists a
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module homomorphism

\pi:V(\varpi_{2})\rightarrow V(\varpi_{2})\oplus V(\varpi_{0})

such that

\pi(b_{(a_{1},a_{2})})\equiv\begin{cases}b_{()}&\text{ if }(a_{1},a_{2})=(1,2),\\
b_{(a_{1},a_{2})}&\text{ if }(a_{1},a_{2})\neq(1,2).\end{cases}

for all
1\leq a_{1}<a_{2}\leq 2n
.

Proof.

The assertion follows from
[
Wat24
, Theorem 4.3.1]
.
Alternatively, one can verify that the vector
w_{0}:=b_{(1,2)}-q^{-2}b_{(3,4)}\in V(\varpi_{2})
spans the
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-submodule isomorphic to
V(\varpi_{0})
(
cf.

[
Wat23b
, Lemma 4.1.4]
).
∎

6.3.
Reduction map and successor map

Definition 6.3.1
.

The
reduction map
is the
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module homomorphism

\operatorname{red}=\operatorname{red}_{l}:V(\varpi_{l})\rightarrow\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq k\leq\min(l,2n-l)\\
l-k\in 2\mathbb{Z}\end{subarray}}V(\varpi_{k})

defined inductively as follows.

(1)

If
l\leq 1
, then the reduction map is the identity map.

(2)

If
l>1
, then the reduction map is the composite

\pi\circ\bigwedge\circ(K^{-1}\otimes\mathrm{id})\circ R\circ(\operatorname{red}_{l-1}\otimes\mathrm{id})\circ R\circ(K\otimes\mathrm{id})\circ\bigvee

Proposition 6.3.2
.

For each
\mathbf{a}\in\mathrm{SST}_{2n}(\varpi_{l})
, we have

\operatorname{red}(b_{\mathbf{a}})\equiv b_{\operatorname{red}(\mathbf{a})}.

Proof.

The assertion follows from Corollary
4.4.3
(
1
), Proposition
6.1.2
, and Lemmas
6.2.3
and
6.2.4
.
∎

Definition 6.3.3
.

Let
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{l})\in\mathrm{Par}_{\leq 2n}
.
Set
\lambda^{\prime}:=(\lambda_{1}-1,\dots,\lambda_{l}-1)
.
The
successor map
is the
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module homomorphism

\operatorname{suc}:V(\lambda)\rightarrow\bigoplus_{\lambda^{\prime}\underset{\text{vert}}{\subseteq}\mu}V(\mu)

defined to be the composite

\operatorname{suc}:=*\circ(\operatorname{red}\otimes\mathrm{id})\circ d.

Proposition 6.3.4
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathrm{Par}_{\leq 2n}
and
T\in\mathrm{SST}_{2n}(\lambda)
.
Then, we have

\operatorname{suc}(b_{T})\equiv b_{\operatorname{suc}(T)}.

Proof.

The assertion follows from Propositions
6.1.2
and
6.3.2
.
∎

6.4.
An orthonormal basis of
V^{\imath}(\nu)

Proposition 6.4.1
.

Let
\nu\in\mathrm{Par}_{\leq n}
.
Then, there exists a basis
\{b^{\imath}_{T}\mid T\in Sp\mathrm{T}_{2n}(\nu)\}
of
V^{\imath}(\nu)
and a
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module homomorphism

p_{\nu}:V(\nu)\rightarrow V^{\imath}(\nu)

such that

p_{\nu}(b_{T})\equiv\begin{cases}b^{\imath}_{T}&\text{ if }T\in Sp\mathrm{T}_{2n}(\nu),\\
0&\text{ if }T\notin Sp\mathrm{T}_{2n}(\nu),\end{cases}

for all
T\in\mathrm{SST}_{2n}(\nu)
.

Proof.

Let
p_{0}:V(\nu)\rightarrow V(\nu)
and
p_{1}:V(\nu)\rightarrow\bigoplus_{\xi\underset{\text{vert}}{\subset}\nu}V(\xi)
denote the composite of the successor map
\operatorname{suc}:V(\nu)\rightarrow\bigoplus_{\xi}V(\xi)
followed by the projections, respectively.
Then, by Corollary
4.3.8
, we have

p_{0}(b_{T})\equiv\begin{cases}b_{T}&\text{ if }T\in Sp\mathrm{T}_{2n}(\nu),\\
0&\text{ if }T\notin Sp\mathrm{T}_{2n}(\nu),\end{cases}\quad p_{1}(b_{T})\equiv\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }T\in Sp\mathrm{T}_{2n}(\nu),\\
b_{\operatorname{suc}(T)}&\text{ if }T\notin Sp\mathrm{T}_{2n}(\nu),\end{cases}

This implies that
p_{0}(V(\nu))
and
p_{1}(V(\nu))
contain linearly independent subsets
\{p_{0}(b_{T})\mid T\in Sp\mathrm{T}_{2n}(\nu)\}
and
\{p_{1}(b_{T})\mid T\notin Sp\mathrm{T}_{2n}(\nu)\}
, respectively.
Since the successor map on
\mathrm{SST}_{2n}(\nu)
is injective by Corollary
4.4.4
, we see that they are bases of the two spaces.

Now, we compute as

\operatorname{ch}_{\imath}p_{0}(V(\nu))=\sum_{T\in Sp\mathrm{T}_{2n}(\nu)}\mathbf{y}^{\operatorname{wt}^{Sp}(T)}=\operatorname{ch}_{\imath}V^{\imath}(\nu).

This implies
p_{0}(\nu)\simeq V^{\imath}(\nu)
.
Thus, we complete the proof.
∎

6.5.
Quantum Littlewood-Richardson map

Definition 6.5.1
.

The quantum Littlewood-Richardson map is the
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module homomorphism

\mathrm{LR}^{A\mathrm{II}}:V(\lambda)\rightarrow\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu\in\mathrm{Par}_{\leq n}\\
\nu\subseteq\lambda\end{subarray}}V^{\imath}(\nu)\otimes\mathbb{Q}(q)\mathrm{Rec}_{2n}(\lambda/\nu)

defined to be the composite of the
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module homomorphism

V(\lambda)\rightarrow\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu\in\mathrm{Par}_{\leq n}\\
\nu\subseteq\lambda\end{subarray}}V(\nu)\otimes\mathbb{Q}(q)\mathrm{SST}_{2n}(\lambda)

which sends
b_{T}
to
\operatorname{suc}^{k}(b_{T})\otimes T
for each
T\in\mathrm{SST}_{2n}(\lambda)
, where
k>0
is such that
\operatorname{suc}^{k}(S)=P^{A\mathrm{II}}(S)
for all
S\in\mathrm{SST}_{2n}(\lambda)
, and the sum of
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-homomorphisms of the form

V(\nu)\otimes\mathbb{Q}(q)\mathrm{SST}_{2n}(\lambda)\rightarrow V^{\imath}(\nu)\otimes\mathbb{Q}(q)\mathrm{Rec}_{2n}(\lambda/\nu)

which sends
b_{S}\otimes T
to
\delta_{S,P^{A\mathrm{II}}(T)}p_{\nu}(b_{P^{A\mathrm{II}}(T)})\otimes Q^{A\mathrm{II}}(T)
for each
S\in\mathrm{SST}_{2n}(\nu)
and
T\in\mathrm{SST}_{2n}(\lambda)
.

The following is immediate from the definition.

Proposition 6.5.2
.

Let
T\in\mathrm{SST}_{2n}(\lambda)
.
Then, we have

\mathrm{LR}^{A\mathrm{II}}(b_{T})\equiv b^{\imath}_{P^{A\mathrm{II}}(T)}\otimes Q^{A\mathrm{II}}(T).

7.
Surjectivity and codomain of the Littlewood-Richardson map

In this section, we complete the proof of the first assertion of Theorem
3.1.4
.

7.1.
Injectivity and codomain of the Littlewood-Richardson map

Proposition 7.1.1
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathrm{Par}_{\leq 2n}
and
T\in\mathrm{SST}_{2n}(\lambda)
.
Then, the tableau
P^{A\mathrm{II}}(T)
is symplectic.
Consequently, we have

\mathrm{LR}^{A\mathrm{II}}(T)\in Sp\mathrm{T}_{2n}(\nu)\times\mathrm{Rec}_{2n}(\lambda/\nu)

for some
\nu\in\mathrm{Par}_{\leq n}
such that
\nu\subseteq\lambda
.

Proof.

Since
\operatorname{suc}(P^{A\mathrm{II}}(T))=P^{A\mathrm{II}}(T)
, it is symplectic by Corollary
4.3.8
.
∎

Let
\lambda,\mu\in\mathrm{Par}_{\leq 2n}
be such that
\mu\subseteq\lambda
.
Define a map

\widetilde{\operatorname{suc}}:\mathrm{SST}_{2n}(\mu)\times\mathrm{Tab}(\lambda/\mu)\rightarrow\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Par}_{\leq 2n}\\
\mu^{\prime}\underset{\text{vert}}{\subseteq}\mu\end{subarray}}\mathrm{SST}_{2n}(\mu^{\prime})\times\mathrm{Tab}(\lambda/\mu^{\prime})

as follows.
Let
(S,R)\in\mathrm{SST}_{2n}(\mu)\times\mathrm{Tab}(\lambda/\mu)
.
Set

k:=\max\{R(i,j)\mid(i,j)\in D(\lambda/\mu)\},

and
\mu^{\prime}:=\mathrm{sh}(\operatorname{suc}(S))
.
Then,

\widetilde{\operatorname{suc}}(S,R)=(\operatorname{suc}(S),R^{\prime}),

where
R^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Tab}(\lambda/\mu^{\prime})
is such that

R^{\prime}(i,j)=\begin{cases}R(i,j)&\text{ if }(i,j)\notin D(\mu),\\
k+1&\text{ if }(i,j)\in D(\mu),\end{cases}\ \text{ for all }(i,j)\in D(\lambda/\mu^{\prime}).

Lemma 7.1.2
.

Let
\lambda,\mu\in\mathrm{Par}_{\leq 2n}
be such that
\mu\subseteq\lambda
.
Then, the map
\widetilde{suc}
on
\mathrm{SST}_{2n}(\mu)\times\mathrm{Tab}(\lambda/\mu)
is injective.

Proof.

Let
(S_{1},R_{1}),(S_{2},R_{2})\in\mathrm{SST}_{2n}(\mu)\times\mathrm{Tab}(\lambda/\mu)
be such that

(\operatorname{suc}(S_{1}),R^{\prime}_{1}):=\widetilde{\operatorname{suc}}(S_{1},R_{1})=\widetilde{\operatorname{suc}}(S_{2},R_{2})=(\operatorname{suc}(S_{2}),R^{\prime}_{2}).

Then, for all
(i,j)\in D(\lambda/\mu)
, we have

R_{1}(i,j)=R^{\prime}_{1}(i,j)=R^{\prime}_{2}(i,j)=R_{2}(i,j).

This implies that

R_{1}=R_{2}.

Next, we show that
S_{1}=S_{2}
.
Let us write

d(S_{i})=(\mathbf{a}_{i},S^{\prime}_{i})\ \text{ for each }i=1,2.

Then, we have

\operatorname{red}(\mathbf{a}_{1})*S^{\prime}_{1}=\operatorname{suc}(S_{1})=\operatorname{suc}(S_{2})=\operatorname{red}(\mathbf{a}_{2})*S^{\prime}_{2}.

Since
|\mathrm{sh}(S^{\prime}_{1})|=|\mathrm{sh}(S^{\prime}_{2})|
, we must have

|\operatorname{red}(\mathbf{a}_{1})|=|\operatorname{red}(\mathbf{a}_{2})|.

Then, by Proposition
2.3.4
, we obtain

(\operatorname{red}(\mathbf{a}_{1}),S^{\prime}_{1})=(\operatorname{red}(\mathbf{a}_{2}),S^{\prime}_{2}).

Now, Corollary
4.4.3
(
2
) implies

\mathbf{a}_{1}=\mathbf{a}_{2}.

Hence, we deduce

S_{1}=\mathbf{a}_{1}*S^{\prime}_{1}=\mathbf{a}_{2}*S^{\prime}_{2}=S_{2},

as desired.
∎

Proposition 7.1.3
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathrm{Par}_{\leq 2n}
.
Then, the Littlewood-Richardson map on
\mathrm{SST}_{2n}(\lambda)
is injective.

Proof.

Let
T_{1},T_{2}\in\mathrm{SST}_{2n}(\lambda)
be such that
\mathrm{LR}^{A\mathrm{II}}(T_{1})=\mathrm{LR}^{A\mathrm{II}}(T_{2})
.
Let us write

\mathrm{LR}^{A\mathrm{II}}(T_{1})=(P,Q),

and set
\nu:=\mathrm{sh}(P)
.

First, note that

\mathrm{LR}^{A\mathrm{II}}(T_{1})=\widetilde{\operatorname{suc}}^{k_{0}}(T_{1},Q^{0})

for a sufficiently large
k_{0}>0
, where
Q^{0}
denotes the unique tableau of shape
\lambda/\lambda
.
For each
k\in[0,k_{0}]
, let
\nu^{k}
denote the partition whose Young diagram is

D(\nu^{k})=D(\nu)\sqcup\{(i,j)\in D(\lambda/\nu)\mid Q(i,j)>k\}.

Then, we see that

\mathrm{sh}(\operatorname{suc}^{k}(T_{1}))=\nu^{k}\ \text{ for all }k\in[0,k_{0}].

Since
\mathrm{LR}^{A\mathrm{II}}(T_{2})=\mathrm{LR}^{A\mathrm{II}}(T_{1})=(P,Q)
, we have also

\mathrm{sh}(\operatorname{suc}^{k}(T_{2}))=\nu^{k}\ \text{ for all }k\in[0,k_{0}].

Applying Lemma
7.1.2
, we deduce that

\operatorname{suc}^{k}(T_{1})=\operatorname{suc}^{k}(T_{2})\ \text{ for all }k\in[0,k_{0}]

by descending induction on
k
.
In particular, we obtain

T_{1}=\operatorname{suc}^{0}(T_{1})=\operatorname{suc}^{0}(T_{2})=T_{2},

as desired.
∎

7.2.
Surjectivity of the Littlewood-Richardson map

Theorem 7.2.1
.

The quantum Littlewood-Richardson map on
V(\lambda)
is a
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module isomorphism.

Proof.

Let
Q\in\mathrm{Rec}_{2n}(\lambda/\nu)
.
Then, there exists
T\in\mathrm{SST}_{2n}(\lambda)
such that
Q^{A\mathrm{II}}(T)=Q
.
By Proposition
6.5.2
, we have

\mathrm{LR}^{A\mathrm{II}}(b_{T})\equiv b_{P^{A\mathrm{II}}(T)}\otimes Q\neq 0.

Since the summand
V^{\imath}(\nu)\otimes\mathbb{Q}(q)Q
is an irreducible
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module, we see that it is contained in the image of
\mathrm{LR}^{A\mathrm{II}}
.
Since the recording tableau
Q
is arbitrary, the
\mathrm{LR}^{A\mathrm{II}}
on
V(\lambda)
is surjective.

The injectivity follows from that of the Littlewood-Richardson map on
\mathrm{SST}_{2n}(\lambda)
and Proposition
6.5.2

∎

Corollary 7.2.2
.

The Littlewood-Richardson map on
\mathrm{SST}_{2n}(\lambda)
is bijective.

8.
Characterization of the recording tableaux

In this section, we prove the second assertion of Theorem
3.1.4
.

8.1.
Some properties of the successor map

Let
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{l})\in\mathrm{Par}_{\leq 2n}
and
T\in\mathrm{SST}_{2n}(\lambda)
.
Let us write
d(T)=(\mathbf{a},S)
,
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{l})
, and
\operatorname{red}(\mathbf{a})=\mathbf{b}=(b_{1},\dots,b_{k})
.

For each
t\in[0,k]
, set

S_{t}:=\begin{cases}S&\text{ if }t=0,\\
b_{t}\rightarrow S_{t-1}&\text{ if }t>0,\end{cases}

and
\lambda^{t}:=\mathrm{sh}(S_{t})
.
Let us write

\operatorname{br}(b_{t},S_{t-1})=(r_{t,1},r_{t,2},\dots,r_{t,s_{t}}).

Also, let
r_{t,0}\in[1,l]
be such that

a_{r_{t,0}}=b_{t}.

Set

\mathrm{br}_{\leq t}:=\{(r_{u,j},j)\mid u\in[1,t],\ j\in[1,s_{u}]\}.

Set
T^{\prime}:=\operatorname{suc}(T)
,
\mu:=\mathrm{sh}(T^{\prime})
, and
l^{\prime}:=\ell(\mu)
.

Define
\mathbf{a}^{\prime}=(a^{\prime}_{1},\dots,a^{\prime}_{l^{\prime}})
,
S^{\prime}
,
\mathbf{b}^{\prime}=(b^{\prime}_{1},\dots,b^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}})
,
S^{\prime}_{t^{\prime}}
,
\mu^{t^{\prime}}
,
(r^{\prime}_{t^{\prime},1},\dots,r^{\prime}_{t^{\prime},s^{\prime}_{t^{\prime}}})
for
t^{\prime}\in[1,k^{\prime}]
in the same way as before.

Lemma 8.1.1
.

Let
t\in[1,k]
be such that
r_{t,0}=r_{t,1}
and
a^{\prime}_{r_{t,1}+1}=a^{\prime}_{r_{t,1}}+1
.
Then, we have
a_{r_{t,0}+1}=a_{r_{t,0}}+1
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.6.3
(
2
), we have

a^{\prime}_{r_{t,1}}=T^{\prime}(r_{t,1},1)=T(r_{t,0},1)=a_{r_{t,0}}.

First, suppose that
(r_{t,1}+1,1)\notin\mathrm{br}_{\leq k}
.
Then, we have

T^{\prime}(r_{t,1}+1,1)=T(r_{t,1}+1,2).

Hence, we deduce that

a^{\prime}_{r_{t,1}+1}=T^{\prime}(r_{t,1}+1,1)=T(r_{t,1}+1,2)\geq T(r_{t,1}+1,1)=a_{r_{t,1}+1}.

This implies that

a_{r_{t,0}+1}=a_{r_{t,1}+1}\leq a^{\prime}_{r_{t,1}+1}=a^{\prime}_{r_{t,1}}+1=a_{r_{t,0}}+1.

Therefore, the assertion follows.

Next, suppose that
(r_{t,1}+1,1)\in\mathrm{br}_{\leq k}
.
Let us write
r_{t,1}+1=r_{u,1}
for some
u\in[1,k]
.
Then, we have

T^{\prime}(r_{t,1}+1,1)=T^{\prime}(r_{u,1},1)=T(r_{u,0},1).

This implies that

a^{\prime}_{r_{t,1}+1}=T^{\prime}(r_{t,1}+1,1)=T(r_{u,0},1)=a_{r_{u,0}}.

Since we have

r_{1,1}<r_{2,1}<\cdots<r_{k,1}

by Lemma
2.6.3
(
4
),
it must hold that
u>t
.
Noting that
a_{r_{u,0}}>a_{r_{t,0}}
, we obtain as before that

a_{r_{u,0}}=a_{r_{t,0}}+1.

This implies that
r_{u,0}=r_{t,0}+1
since
(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})
is a strictly increasing sequence.
Hence, the assertion follows.
∎

Lemma 8.1.2
.

Let
t\in[0,k]
.
Then, we have

\sharp\{t^{\prime}\in[1,k^{\prime}]\mid r^{\prime}_{t^{\prime},0}\leq r_{t,1}\}\geq t,

where we set
r_{0,1}:=0
.
In particular, it holds that
k^{\prime}\geq k
.

Proof.

For each
t\in[0,k]
, set

N(t):=\sharp\{t^{\prime}\in[1,k^{\prime}]\mid r^{\prime}_{t^{\prime},0}\leq r_{t,1}\}.

Note that we have

N(t)=\sharp\{i\in[1,r_{t,1}]\mid a^{\prime}_{i}\notin\operatorname{rem}(\mathbf{a}^{\prime})\}=r_{t,1}-|(a^{\prime}_{1},\dots,a^{\prime}_{r_{t,1}})\cap\operatorname{rem}(\mathbf{a}^{\prime})|.

Let us prove the assertion by induction on
t
.
When
t=0
, the assertion is trivial.
Assume that
t>0
and the assertion holds for
0,1,\dots,t-1
.

First, suppose that
a^{\prime}_{r_{t,1}}\notin\operatorname{rem}(\mathbf{a}^{\prime})
.
Then, we have

N(t)\geq N(t-1)+1.

Hence, we deduce from our induction hypothesis that

N(t)\geq N(t-1)+1\geq(t-1)+1=t,

as desired.

Next, suppose that
a^{\prime}_{r_{t,1}}\in\operatorname{rem}(\mathbf{a}^{\prime})
and
a^{\prime}_{r_{t,1}}\notin 2\mathbb{Z}
.
Then, by Proposition
4.2.2
, we must have
r_{t,1}<l
,
a^{\prime}_{r_{t,1}+1}=a^{\prime}_{r_{t,1}}+1
, and

a^{\prime}_{r_{t,1}}<2r_{t,1}-|\operatorname{rem}(a^{\prime}_{1},\dots,a^{\prime}_{r_{t,1}-1})|.

Also, by Corollary
4.2.8
, we have

|\operatorname{rem}(a^{\prime}_{1},\dots,a^{\prime}_{r_{t,1}-1})|\geq 2r_{t,1}-a^{\prime}_{r_{t,1}}-1.

Hence, we obtain

|\operatorname{rem}(a^{\prime}_{1},\dots,a^{\prime}_{r_{t,1}-1})|=2r_{t,1}-a^{\prime}_{r_{t,1}}-1.

Therefore,

N(t)=r_{t,1}-(|\operatorname{rem}(a^{\prime}_{1},\dots,a^{\prime}_{r_{t,1}-1})|+1)=a^{\prime}_{r_{t,1}}-r_{t,1}.

On the other hand, by Corollary
4.2.8
again, we have

|\operatorname{rem}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r_{t,0}-1})|\geq 2r_{t,0}-a_{r_{t,0}}-1.

Also, since
a_{r_{t,0}}\notin\operatorname{rem}(\mathbf{a})
, it holds that

r_{t,0}-|\operatorname{rem}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r_{t,0}-1})|=t.

Hence,

a_{r_{t,0}}-r_{t,0}\geq t-1.

Combining above, we obtain

N(t)=a^{\prime}_{r_{t,1}}-r_{t,0}+(r_{t,0}-r_{t,1})\geq t+(r_{t,0}-r_{t,1})-1.

Therefore, the assertion follows when
r_{t,0}>r_{t,1}
.
Hence, we only need to consider the case when
r_{t,0}=r_{t,1}
.
In this case, Lemma
8.1.1
implies

a_{r_{t,0}+1}=a_{r_{t,0}}+1.

Since
a_{r_{t,0}}\notin\operatorname{rem}(\mathbf{a})
, we have

a_{r_{t,0}}\geq 2r_{t,0}-|\operatorname{rem}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r_{t,0}-1})|=r_{t,0}+t.

Now, we deduce

N(t)=a^{\prime}_{r_{t,1}}-r_{t,1}=a_{r_{t,0}}-r_{t,0}\geq t,

as desired.

Finally, suppose that
a^{\prime}_{r_{t,1}}\in\operatorname{rem}(\mathbf{a}^{\prime})
and
a^{\prime}_{r_{t,1}}\in 2\mathbb{Z}
.
In this case, one can deduce the assertion in a similar way to the previous case.

Thus, we complete the proof.
∎

Lemma 8.1.3
.

Let
t\in[0,k]
.
Then, we have
s^{\prime}_{t}\geq s_{t}
and
r^{\prime}_{t,j-1}\leq r_{t,j}
for all
j\in[1,s_{t}]
, where we set
s_{0}=s^{\prime}_{0}=0
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
t
.
The case when
t=0
is trivial.
Hence, assume that
t>0
and the assertion holds for
0,1,\dots,t-1
.

We prove the assertion by induction on
j
.
By lemma
8.1.2
and the fact that
r^{\prime}_{1,0}<r^{\prime}_{2,0}<\cdots<r^{\prime}_{k^{\prime},0}
, we obtain

r^{\prime}_{t,0}\leq r_{t,1}.

Now, assume that
j>1
and we have
s^{\prime}_{t},s_{t}\geq j-1
and
r^{\prime}_{t,j-2}\leq r_{t,j-1}
.
When
s_{t}=j-1
, there is nothing to prove.
Hence, assume that
s_{t}\geq j
.

By Lemma
2.6.3
, we have

r^{\prime}_{t,j-1}=\min\{r^{\prime}\in[1,\mathrm{col}_{j-1}(\mu^{t-1})+1]\mid T^{\prime}(r^{\prime},j)\geq T^{\prime}(r^{\prime}_{t,j-2},j-1)\}.

Note that our induction hypothesis implies that

r^{\prime}_{t-1,j-1}\leq r_{t-1,j}.

The right-hand side is less than
r_{t,j}
by Lemma
2.6.3
(
4
).
Also we have

r_{t,j}\leq\mathrm{col}_{j}(\lambda^{t-1})+1,

and

\mathrm{col}_{j}(\lambda^{t-1})\leq\mathrm{col}_{j}(\lambda^{k})=\mathrm{col}_{j}(\mu^{0})\leq\mathrm{col}_{j-1}(\mu^{0})\leq\mathrm{col}_{j-1}(\mu^{t-1}).

Observe that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&T^{\prime}(r_{t,j},j)=T(r_{t,j-1},j),\\
&T^{\prime}(r^{\prime}_{t,j-2},j-1)=\begin{cases}T(r_{u,j-2},j-1)&\text{ if }r^{\prime}_{t,j-2}=r_{u,j-1}\text{ for some }u\in[1,k],\\
T(r^{\prime}_{t,j-2},j)&\text{ otherwise},\end{cases}\end{split}

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}&T(r_{u,j-2},j-1)\leq T(r_{u,j-1},j)\leq T(r_{t,j-1},j),\\
&T(r^{\prime}_{t,j-2},j)\leq T(r_{t,j-1},j).\end{split}

By above, we obtain
r^{\prime}_{t,j-1}\leq r_{t,j}
and
s^{\prime}_{t}\geq j
, as desired.
∎

Proposition 8.1.4
.

Let
r\in[0,l]
.
Then, we have

\sharp\{t\in[1,k]\mid r_{t,s_{t}}\leq r\}\leq\sharp\{t^{\prime}\in[1,k^{\prime}]\mid r^{\prime}_{t^{\prime},s_{t^{\prime}}}\leq r\}.

Proof.

For each
t\in[1,k]
, we have
s^{\prime}_{t}\geq s_{t}
and
r^{\prime}_{t,s_{t}-1}\leq r_{t,s_{t}}
.
Hence, if
r_{t,s_{t}}\leq r
, then

r^{\prime}_{t,s^{\prime}_{t}}\leq r^{\prime}_{t,s_{t}-1}\leq r_{t,s_{t}}\leq r.

Thus, the assertion follows.
∎

8.2.
Symplectic Littlewood-Richardson tableaux

Definition 8.2.1
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathrm{Par}_{\leq 2n}
and
\nu\in\mathrm{Par}_{\leq n}
.
A tableau
T\in\mathrm{Tab}(\lambda/\nu)
is said to be a
symplectic Littlewood-Richardson tableau
if it satisfies the following.

(1)

T\in\mathrm{SST}_{2n}(\lambda/\nu)
.

(2)

Let
(w_{1},\dots,w_{N})
denote the column-word
w^{\mathrm{col}}(T)
of
T
.
Then, the reversed word
(w_{N},\dots,w_{1})
is a lattice permutation:
For each
r\in[1,N]
and
k\in[1,2n-1]
, the number of occurrences of
k
in the subsequence
(w_{N},\dots,w_{r})
is greater than or equal to that of
k+1
.

(3)

The sequence
\operatorname{wt}(T)=(T[1],T[2],\dots,T[2n])
is a partition which has even columns (see Definition
2.1.1
).

(4)

If
T(i,j)=2k+1
for some
(i,j)\in D(\lambda/\nu)
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, then we have
i\leq n+k
.

Let
\mathrm{LRT}^{Sp}_{2n}(\lambda/\nu)
denote the set of all symplectic Littlewood-Richardson tableaux of shape
\lambda/\nu
.

Remark 8.2.2
.

Let
T\in\mathrm{SST}_{2n}(\lambda/\nu)
be such that the sequence
\operatorname{wt}(T)
is a partition which has even columns.
Then, we have
T\in\mathrm{LRT}^{Sp}_{2n}(\lambda/\nu)
if and only if the reversed column-word
fits
\lambda/\nu

n
-symplectically
in the sense of
[
Sun90
, Definition 3.9]
.

The symplectic Littlewood-Richardson tableaux provide us a branching rule:

Theorem 8.2.3

(
cf.

[
Sun90
, Corollary 3.12]
)
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathrm{Par}_{\leq 2n}
and
\nu\in\mathrm{Par}_{\leq n}
be such that
\nu\subseteq\lambda
.
Then, we have

m_{\lambda,\nu}=|\mathrm{LRT}^{Sp}_{2n}(\lambda/\nu)|.

8.3.
Characterization of the recording tableaux

Let
\lambda\in\mathrm{Par}_{\leq 2n}
,
\nu\in\mathrm{Par}_{\leq n}
be such that
\nu\subseteq\lambda
.

Lemma 8.3.1
.

We have

\mathrm{Rec}_{2n}(\lambda/\nu)\subseteq\widetilde{\mathrm{Rec}}_{2n}(\lambda/\nu).

Proof.

Let
Q\in\mathrm{Rec}_{2n}(\lambda/\nu)
.
We only need to show that
Q\in\widetilde{\mathrm{Rec}}_{2n}(\lambda/\nu)
, that is, to verify that the tableau
Q
satisfies conditions (R1)–(R5) in Section
3
.

By the definition of recording tableaux, there exists
T\in\mathrm{SST}_{2n}(\lambda)
such that
Q^{A\mathrm{II}}(T)=Q
.
For each
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, set

P^{k}:=\begin{cases}T&\text{ if }k=0,\\
\operatorname{suc}(P^{k-1})&\text{ if }k>0,\end{cases}\quad\nu^{k}:=\mathrm{sh}(P^{k}).

Let us write

d(P^{k})=(\mathbf{a}^{k},S^{k}),\ \mathbf{a}^{k}=(a^{k}_{1},\dots,a^{k}_{l_{k}}),\ \operatorname{red}(\mathbf{a}^{k})=\mathbf{b}^{k}=(b^{k}_{1},\dots,b^{k}_{l^{\prime}_{k}}),

and

\mathrm{br}(b^{k}_{t},b^{k}_{t-1}\rightarrow(\cdots\rightarrow(b^{k}_{1}\rightarrow S^{k-1})))=(r^{k}_{t,1},r^{k}_{t,2},\dots,r^{k}_{t,s^{k}_{t}}).

First, let us verify conditions (R1) and (R2).
Let
(i,j)\in D(\lambda/\nu)
and write
Q(i,j)=k
.
Suppose that
(i,j+1)\in D(\lambda/\nu)
(resp.,
(i+1,j)\in D(\lambda/\nu)
).
Then, since
\nu^{k}\underset{\text{vert}}{\subseteq}\nu^{k-1}
, we must have
(i,j+1)\notin D(\nu^{k-1})
(resp.,
(i+1,j)\notin D(\nu^{k})
).
This, together with Lemma
3.1.2
, implies that
Q(i,j+1)\leq k-1
(resp.,
Q(i+1,j)\leq k
), as desired.

Next, let us verify conditions (R3) and (R4).
Let
k>0
.
Then, we have

Q[k]=|\nu^{k-1}/\nu^{k}|

by Lemma
3.1.2
.
Since
P^{k}=\operatorname{suc}(P^{k-1})=\operatorname{red}(\mathbf{a}^{k-1})*S^{k-1}
, it holds that

|\nu^{k-1}/\nu^{k}|=|\operatorname{rem}(\mathbf{a}^{k-1})|.

The right-hand side is even by Lemma
4.2.3
, and is greater than or equal to
2(\ell(\nu^{k-1})-n)
by Proposition
4.2.7
.

Finally, let us verify condition (R5).
Let
r,k>0
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}Q_{\leq r}[k]&=\sharp\{i\in[1,r]\mid Q(i,j)=k\ \text{ for some }j\}\\
&=|[1,r]\setminus\{r^{k-1}_{t,s^{k-1}_{t}}\mid t\in[1,l^{\prime}_{k-1}]\}|\\
&=r-\sharp\{t\in[1,l^{\prime}_{k-1}]\mid r^{k-1}_{t,s^{k-1}_{t}}\leq r\}.\end{split}

Hence, we deduce that

Q_{\leq r}[k]-Q_{\leq r}[k+1]=\{t^{\prime}\in[1,l^{\prime}_{k}]\mid r^{k}_{t^{\prime},s^{k}_{t^{\prime}}}\leq r\}-\{t\in[1,l^{\prime}_{k-1}]\mid r^{k-1}_{t,s^{k-1}_{t}}\leq r\}.

The right-hand side is nonnegative by Proposition
8.1.4
.

Thus, we complete the proof.
∎

Lemma 8.3.2
.

Consider the map

\widetilde{\mathrm{Rec}}_{2n}(\lambda/\nu)\rightarrow\mathrm{Tab}(\lambda/\nu)

which sends each
R\in\widetilde{\mathrm{Rec}}_{2n}(\lambda/\nu)
to the tableaux of shape
\lambda/\nu
whose
(i,j)
entry is equal to
R_{\leq i}[R(i,j)]
; the number of occurrences of
R(i,j)
in
R
in the
i
-th row or above.
Then, it is injective and its image is contained in
\mathrm{LRT}^{Sp}_{2n}(\lambda/\nu)
.

Proof.

Let
R\in\widetilde{\mathrm{Rec}}_{2n}(\lambda/\nu)
, and
S\in\mathrm{Tab}(\lambda/\nu)
be the image of
R
under the map above.
We only need to show that the tableau
S
satisfies the conditions (
1
)–(
4
) in Definition
8.2.1
.

First, let us verify condition (
1
).
By condition (R1), each
k^{\prime}>0
appears as an entry of
R
at most once in each row.
In particular, we have

R[k^{\prime}]\leq\ell(\lambda)\leq 2n\ \text{ for all }k^{\prime}>0.

This implies that the entries of
S
are in
[1,2n]
.

Let
(i,j)\in D(\lambda/\nu)
and set
k:=R(i,j)
.
Suppose that
(i,j+1)\in D(\lambda/\nu)
(resp.,
(i+1,j)\in D(\lambda/\nu)
), and set
k^{\prime}:=R(i,j+1)<k
(resp.,
k^{\prime\prime}:=R(i+1,j)\leq k
).
Here, we used condition (R1) (resp., (R2)).
Then, we have

S(i,j+1)=R_{\leq i}[k^{\prime}]\geq R_{\leq i}[k]=S(i,j),

(resp.,

S(i+1,j)=R_{\leq i+1}[k^{\prime\prime}]=R_{\leq i}[r^{\prime\prime}]+1\geq R_{\leq i}[r]+1=S(i,j)+1,)

where the inequality follows from condition (R5).
This implies that
S
is semistandard.

Second, let us verify condition (
2
).
Let us write

w_{\mathrm{col}}(S)=(w_{1},\dots,w_{N})\text{ and }w_{\mathrm{col}}(R)=(w^{\prime}_{1},\dots,w^{\prime}_{N}).

Let
r\in[1,N]
and
k\in[1,2n]
.
Then, we have

\sharp\{t\in[r,N]\mid w_{t}=k\}=\sharp\{k^{\prime}>0\mid\sharp\{t^{\prime}\in[r,N]\mid w^{\prime}_{t^{\prime}}=k^{\prime}\}\geq k\}.

The right-hand side decreases as
k
increases.
Hence, the assertion follows.

Next, let us verify condition (
3
).
By condition (R5), we have

R[1]\geq R[2]\geq\cdots.

Then, we can consider the tableau whose
j
-th column consists of exactly
R[j]

j
’s.
Clearly, its shape is
\operatorname{wt}(S)
.
Noting that
R[j]\in 2\mathbb{Z}
by condition (R3), we see that the partition
\operatorname{wt}(S)
has even columns.

Finally, let us verify condition (
4
).
Let
(i,j)\in D(\lambda/\nu)
and suppose that
S(i,j)=2k+1
for some
k\geq 0
.
When
i\leq n
, there is nothing to prove.
Hence, assume that
i>n
.

Set
r:=R(i,j)
.
Then, we have
R_{\leq i}[r]=2k+1
.
Let
\mu
be the partition whose Young diagram is given by

D(\mu)=D(\nu)\sqcup\{(i,j)\in D(\lambda/\nu)\mid R(i,j)\geq r\}.

We have
R_{\leq i}[r]\geq 2(i-n)
.
Otherwise, we obtain

R[r]=R_{\leq\ell(\mu)}[r]\leq R_{\leq i}[r]+(\ell(\mu)-i)<2(i-n)+(\ell(\mu)-i)\leq 2(\ell(\mu)-i),

which contradicts condition (R4).

Now, we have

2k+1=R_{\leq i}[r]\geq 2(i-n).

This implies that
2k\geq 2(i-n)
, and then the assertion follows.

Thus, we complete the proof.
∎

Theorem 8.3.3
.

We have
\mathrm{Rec}_{2n}(\lambda/\nu)=\widetilde{\mathrm{Rec}}_{2n}(\lambda/\nu)
.

Proof.

By Lemmas
8.3.1
and
8.3.2
, we have

\mathrm{Rec}_{2n}(\lambda/\nu)\subseteq\widetilde{\mathrm{Rec}}_{2n}(\lambda/\nu)

and

|\mathrm{Rec}_{2n}(\lambda/\nu)|\leq|\widetilde{\mathrm{Rec}}_{2n}(\lambda/\nu)|\leq|\mathrm{LRT}^{Sp}_{2n}(\lambda/\nu)|.

Hence, we only need to show that

(8.1)

\displaystyle|\mathrm{Rec}_{2n}(\lambda/\nu)|=|\mathrm{LRT}^{Sp}_{2n}(\lambda/\nu)|.

Recall from Theorem
8.2.3
that

|\mathrm{LRT}^{Sp}_{2n}(\lambda/\nu)|=m_{\lambda,\nu}.

On the other hand, by Theorem
7.2.1
and Proposition
6.2.2
, we obtain

|\mathrm{Rec}_{2n}(\lambda/\nu)|=m_{\lambda,\nu}.

Therefore, equation (
8.1
) holds.

Thus, we complete the proof.
∎
